Hume explained induction with a theory of the mind[[133]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorrisBrown2021Sec._4-138) that was in part inspired by Newton's theory of gravitation.[[134]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorrisBrown2021Sec._3-139) Popper rejected Hume's explanation and proposed his own mechanism: science progresses by trial and error within an [evolutionary epistemology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_epistemology#Growth_of_knowledge "Evolutionary epistemology"). Hume believed that his psychological induction process follows laws of nature, but that this does not imply the existence of a method of justification based on logical rules. In fact, he argued that any induction mechanism, including the one his theory described could not be justified logically.[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHenderson2018-140) Similarly, Popper adopted an evolutionary epistemology that implies that some laws explain progress, but insists that the process of trial and error is not rigorous and that an element of irrationality is unavoidable. 
Though they be rational, these explanations cannot be turned into methods of justification. This was insufficient for philosophers such as [Bertrand Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bertrand_Russell "Bertrand Russell"), who once expressed the view that if Hume's problem cannot be solved, "there is no intellectual difference between sanity and insanity"[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHenderson2018-140) and discussed what is needed for induction to be possible.[[136]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTERussell1998Chap._VI-141)[[137]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTERussell1948Part_VI,_Sec._II-142) He argued that for induction to be possible, a law to make inferences from matters of fact is needed and such law, unlike the principles of deductive logic, must be synthetic. He said "the only alternative to this hypothesis is complete scepticism".[[138]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTERussell1948189%E2%80%93190-143) Lakatos approved Russell's justificationist view.[[139]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTELakatos1978p._100,_note_1-144) His proposal of sophisticated falsificationism was natural in that context. 
Therefore, Lakatos urged Popper to find an inductive principle behind the trial and error learning process[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZahar1983167-145) and sophisticated falsificationism was his own approach to this challenge.[[141]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTELakatos1978Sec._1.1-146)[[142]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTELakatos1978Sec._1.2.b-147) Kuhn, Feyerabend, Musgrave and others mentioned and Lakatos himself acknowledged that this attempt failed, because no normative methodology existed—Lakatos' methodology was anarchy in disguise.[[143]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTELakatos1978116%E2%80%93117-148)[[144]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMusgrave1976458,_478-149)[[145]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatkins19896-150)[[146]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeyerabend197815-151)[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeyerabend1978b\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidlGMyBwAAQBAJpgPA120_120\]-152)[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeyerabend1981\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidng-l_t5rmNsCpgPA148_148\]-153)[[149]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTECouvalis1997\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidaiznXyM2dc8CpgPA74_74-75\]-154)
### Popperian falsificationism
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=31 "Edit section: Popperian falsificationism")]
Popper's philosophy is sometimes said to fail to recognize the Quine-Duhem thesis, which would make it a form of dogmatic falsificationism. For example, Watkins wrote "apparently forgetting that he had once said 'Duhem is right [...]', Popper set out to devise potential falsifiers just for Newton's fundamental assumptions".[[150]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatkins1984Sec_8.5-155) But, Popper's philosophy is not always qualified of falsificationism in the pejorative manner associated with dogmatic or naive falsificationism.[[151]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChalmers201359-156) The problems of falsification are acknowledged by the falsificationists. For example, Chalmers pointed out that falsificationists freely admit that observation relies on theory.[[152]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChalmers201360-157) Thornton, referring to Popper, says that the predictions inferred from conjectures are not directly compared with the facts simply because all observation statements are theory-laden.[[153]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThornton2016Sec_5-158) For the critical rationalists, the problems of falsification are not an issue, because they do not try to make experimental falsifications logical or to logically justify them, nor to use them to logically explain progress. Instead, they rely on critical discussions around experimental falsifications.[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPopper1972-11) Lakatos made a distinction between a "falsification" (with quotation marks) in Popper's philosophy and a falsification (without quotation marks) that can be used in a systematic methodology where rejections are justified.[[154]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTELakatos197836-159) He knew that Popper's philosophy had never been about this kind of justification, but claimed that it should have been.[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZahar1983167-145) Sometimes, Popper and other falsificationists said that when a theory is falsified it is rejected[[155]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPopper2002aChap._1;_Sec_IX-160)[[156]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller19947-161) (dogmatic falsificationism), but they said that in the general context of critical rationalism, in which all decisions are open to critical discussions and can be revised.[[157]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarcia200630-162) Popper complained that his discussions of rejection are often taken out of context. 
## Controversies
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=32 "Edit section: Controversies")]
### Creativity versus induction
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=33 "Edit section: Creativity versus induction")]
Main article: [The problem of induction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_problem_of_induction "The problem of induction")
As discussed in the section [§ Naive falsificationism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#Naive_falsificationism), Lakatos and Popper agreed that universal scientific laws cannot be derived logically, except from broader laws that encompass them. However, unlike Popper, Lakatos believed that induction was the only alternative to deduction. He encouraged Popper to explicitly adopt an inductive approach[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZahar1983167-145) and sought such an inductive method.[[158]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTELakatos1978112-163) However, Lakatos' method never provided precise inductive rules. In response to critiques from [Thomas Kuhn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Kuhn "Thomas Kuhn"), [Paul Feyerabend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Feyerabend "Paul Feyerabend"), and [Alan Musgrave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Musgrave "Alan Musgrave"), Lakatos admitted that his methodology relied on scientists' judgment.[[143]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTELakatos1978116%E2%80%93117-148) Feyerabend, in _Against Method_ , argued that Lakatos' methodology was essentially epistemological anarchism in disguise,[[159]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMusgrave1976458-164)[[145]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatkins19896-150)[[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMusgrave1976478-165) a view echoed by Musgrave.[[159]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMusgrave1976458-164) Later, Feyerabend noted that Lakatos proposed rules, but these rules did not specify when they must be applied and Feyerabend maintained his position.[[146]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeyerabend197815-151)[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeyerabend1978b\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidlGMyBwAAQBAJpgPA120_120\]-152)[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeyerabend1981\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidng-l_t5rmNsCpgPA148_148\]-153)
Popper also proposed a methodology with rules, but these were non-inductive, as they did not independently confirm the validity of scientific laws. Instead, they relied on scientists' creativity or judgment to determine which theories to study, identify significant problems, and propose testable hypotheses.[[161]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZahar1983168-166) Citing Einstein, Popper argued that this creative process eliminated the need for an inductive methodology or a logical pathway to discover scientific laws.[[162]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPopper2002Sec._Elimination_of_Psychologism-167)[[163]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeldmanWilliams2007\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidRaK1SU2KzwACpgPA151_151\]-168)[[164]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEYehuda2018\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidrsY9DwAAQBAJpgPA41_41\]-169)
### Ahistorical versus historiographical
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=34 "Edit section: Ahistorical versus historiographical")]
Main article: [Imre Lakatos § Research programmes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imre_Lakatos#Research_programmes "Imre Lakatos")
Lakatos' methodology built significantly on Popper's by incorporating a historical perspective. Lakatos supported his methodology with examples from the history of science. He defined what he called research programmes, that can be either pursued or abandoned. Research programmes are classified as progressive or degenerative; the latter are eventually abandoned. Lakatos claimed that this classification is largely supported by historical evidence. In contrast, Popper did not design his methodology to rigorously analyze the history of science. However, he occasionally called on historical examples. For instance, he noted that highly successful scientific theories were often disproven. He also introduced, before Lakatos, the notion of metaphysical research programs.[[165]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPopper1974133%E2%80%93143-170)[[166]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTENola2013-171) He never intended to use them in a rigorous inductive methodology, but in a text that inspired Lakatos he discussed how they change over time.[[167]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkrami2009-172) Later, he compared them to Kuhn's paradigms.[[167]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAkrami2009-172) Lakatos removed the "metaphysical" part and sought rigorous inductive rules. Later, he abandoned this research and adopted rules that require judgment. According to Eli Zahar, Lakatos "admitted that the difference between his position and Popper's was so small as to become purely verbal".[[168]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZahar2007-173)
### State of affairs as falsifier versus actual falsification
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=35 "Edit section: State of affairs as falsifier versus actual falsification")]
In 1974, Lakatos challenged Popper to demonstrate that his theory of falsifiability was itself falsifiable, asking, "Under what conditions would you abandon your demarcation criterion?"[[169]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTELakatos1974245-174) Popper responded, "I shall abandon my theory if Professor Lakatos succeeds in showing that Newton's theory is no more falsifiable by observable states of affairs than Freud's."[[170]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPopper19741010-175) In Popper's terminology, a "falsifier" is not an actual observation, but instead a hypothetical situation, a state of affairs, a logical concept that as such is not open to evasive methodological explanations. For example, Newton's law of gravitation states that a brick falls downward. A hypothetical observation that the brick falls upward is a falsifier (demonstrating falsifiability) even if something else such as strings attached to the brick could explain the observation.[[171]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPopper1974105%E2%80%93109-176)
In 1982, [David Stove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Stove "David Stove") criticized Popper, claiming that Lakatos' challenge was successful. Stove contended that any observation appearing to contradict Newtonian physics could be explained by other laws or mechanisms, suggesting no truly "non-Newtonian" behavior was possible. Stove claimed that Popper's counterexamples, such as missiles following a "non-Newtonian trajectory" or objects not falling due to obvious counteracting forces, were either flawed (e.g., [begging the question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Begging_the_question "Begging the question")) or consistent with Newtonian physics.[[172]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStove198292-177) Popper reiterated that his falsifiability criterion was a purely logical concept, distinct from the practical ability to disprove a theory. He wrote, "An entire literature rests on the failure to observe this distinction."[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPopper1983Introduction_1982-2)
### Routine versus revolutionary science
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=36 "Edit section: Routine versus revolutionary science")]
Main article: [Paradigm shift](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paradigm_shift "Paradigm shift")
Kuhn examined periods of normal science and the transitions (revolutions), that separate them.[[173]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuhn1996-178) Popper was interested only in revolutions.[[174]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuhn1974802-179)[[175]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatkins197028-180) He claimed that the purpose of science, mathematics, and metaphysics–all forms of knowledge—is to address and solve problems.[[176]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPopper1994155%E2%80%93156-181) Kuhn noted that during periods of normal science, scientists use established theories to routinely solve problems, questioning their validity only when the theory fails. This perspective aligns with Popper's view of problem solving, but places more emphasis on solving routine problems that do not challenge the underlying theory. Kuhn claimed that Popper focused too heavily on formal or logical falsifications and did not adequately explain the social and informal aspects of scientific progress. 
### Unfalsifiability versus falsity of astrology
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=37 "Edit section: Unfalsifiability versus falsity of astrology")]
Main article: [Astrology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrology "Astrology")
Popper often used astrology as an example of a [pseudoscience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pseudoscience "Pseudoscience"). He says that it is not falsifiable because both the theory itself and its predictions are too imprecise.[[88]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPopper2002a37-90) Kuhn remarked that many predictions made by astrologers were quite precise and were often falsified.[[177]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuhn1970\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidVutfm5n6LKYCpgPA7_7%E2%80%938\]-182)
### Epistemological anarchism vs the scientific method
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=38 "Edit section: Epistemological anarchism vs the scientific method")]
Main article: [Epistemological anarchism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistemological_anarchism "Epistemological anarchism")
Feyeraband entirely rejected prescriptive methodology, including Lakatos' argument for [_ad hoc_ hypotheses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ad_hoc_hypothesis "Ad hoc hypothesis"), arguing that science could not have progressed without making use of every available method. He rejected reliance on a scientific method, along with any special authority for science that might derive from such a method.[[178]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin2017-183) He said that the only possible universally valid methodological rule was [epistemological anarchism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistemological_anarchism "Epistemological anarchism") (anything goes) was the only candidate.[[179]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeyerabend1993-184) Ultimately, he claimed that science's special status derives from the value of the results rather than its method.[[180]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBroad1979-185)
### Sokal and Bricmont
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=39 "Edit section: Sokal and Bricmont")]
In _[Fashionable Nonsense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fashionable_Nonsense "Fashionable Nonsense"),_ physicists [Alan Sokal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Sokal "Alan Sokal") and [Jean Bricmont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Bricmont "Jean Bricmont") criticise falsifiability on several grounds but especially for its disregard of the importance of predictions: 
> ...the history of science teaches us that scientific theories come to be accepted above all because of their successes. For example, on the basis of [Newtonian mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_mechanics "Classical mechanics"), physicists have been able to deduce a great number of both astronomical and terrestrial motions, in excellent agreement with observations. Moreover, the credibility of Newtonian mechanics was reinforced by correct predictions such as the [return of Halley's comet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halley%27s_Comet#Computation_of_orbit "Halley's Comet") in 1759 and by spectacular successes such as [finding Neptune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_of_Neptune "Discovery of Neptune") in 1846 where [Le Verrier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbain_Le_Verrier "Urbain Le Verrier") and [Adams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Couch_Adams "John Couch Adams") predicted it should be.[[181]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-186)
Sokal and Bricmont also argue that the problems of falsifiability gave "rise to a strongly irrationalist reaction"[[182]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-187) of [postmodernist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postmodernism "Postmodernism") [epistemological relativism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistemological_relativism "Epistemological relativism").[[183]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller2000-188)
## Falsification and open science
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=40 "Edit section: Falsification and open science")]
### Falsifiability and research integrity
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=41 "Edit section: Falsifiability and research integrity")]
At the core of Popper's falsification principle, Karl Popper's principle of falsification centers on figuring out what specific observation would prove a hypothesis wrong, and then trying to find it. The research process, therefore, is ideally structured to test a hypothesis by seeking the specific result that would falsify it. If a prediction is observed, the hypothesis is corroborated (supported); repeated failures to observe the predicted result may lead to its rejection in a critical discussion. 
### Compromised methodology
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=42 "Edit section: Compromised methodology")]
However, numerous authors have highlighted that due to the [reproducibility crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Replication_crisis "Replication crisis"),[[184]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-189) the rejection is compromised by poor practices through the research process, including vague or ill-defined hypotheses,[[185]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-190)[[186]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-191) inadequate or under-reported data processing,[[187]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-192)[[188]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-193) and problematic data analysis.[[189]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-194)[[190]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-195) The issue is that the methodology maybe negatively influenced by the [researcher degrees of freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Researcher_degrees_of_freedom "Researcher degrees of freedom") in which the procedures are carried out. 
### Open science solutions
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=43 "Edit section: Open science solutions")]
The [Open Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_science "Open science") movement has introduced valuable tools and practices aimed at mitigating these biases and restoring the integrity required for meaningful falsification. By increasing transparency and rigor, Open Science measures help to reduce researcher degrees of freedom and improve rejection of wrong hypotheses. Key tools and practices include: 
  * [Pre-registration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preregistration_\(science\) "Preregistration \(science\)"): researchers commit to their hypotheses, methods, and analysis plans _before_ data collection. This eliminates the possibility of _post hoc_ (after the fact) changes and prevents the selective reporting of results, known as [cherry picking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cherry_picking "Cherry picking").[[191]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-196)
  * [Open data](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_data "Open data") and open methods: the sharing of data and materials facilitate independent attempts to replicate findings, which directly tests the robustness and potential falsity of previous results and help contribute to reduce biases for assessing biases.[[192]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-197)[[193]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-198)
  * [Reporting standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reporting_standards_in_the_scientific_literature "Reporting standards in the scientific literature"): checklists and standardized guidelines improve the quality and completeness of research reports, ensuring all procedural steps and data analyses are transparently detailed,[[194]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-199)[[195]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-200)[[196]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-201)[[197]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-202)[[198]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-203)[[199]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-204) allowing others to fully assess the study's adherence to falsification standards.[[200]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-205)


## See also
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=44 "Edit section: See also")]
  * [Black swan theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_swan_theory "Black swan theory") – Theory of response to surprise events
  * [Contingency (philosophy)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contingency_\(philosophy\) "Contingency \(philosophy\)") – Possible truths which are not necessary
  * [Defeasible reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defeasible_reasoning "Defeasible reasoning") – Reasoning that is rationally compelling, though not deductively valid
  * [Deniable encryption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deniable_encryption "Deniable encryption") – Encryption techniques where an adversary cannot prove that the plaintext data exists - claim that a ciphertext decrypts to a particular plaintext can be falsified by possible decryption to another potential plaintext
  * [Fallibilism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallibilism "Fallibilism") – Philosophical principle
  * [Metaphysical solipsism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysical_solipsism "Metaphysical solipsism") – Variety of philosophical idealism
  * [Methodological solipsism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodological_solipsism "Methodological solipsism") – Term in epistemology and philosophy of mind
  * [Not even wrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Not_even_wrong "Not even wrong") – English phrase
  * [Philosophical razor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophical_razor "Philosophical razor") – Principle that allows one to eliminate unlikely explanations 
    * [Mike Alder § Newton's Flaming Laser Sword](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Alder#Newton's_Flaming_Laser_Sword "Mike Alder")
    * [Occam's razor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occam%27s_razor "Occam's razor") – Philosophical problem-solving principle
  * [Philosophy of mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy_of_mathematics "Philosophy of mathematics")
  * [Plausible deniability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plausible_deniability "Plausible deniability") – Ability to deny responsibility
  * [Pragmatic maxim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pragmatic_maxim "Pragmatic maxim") – Maxim of logic formulated by Charles Sanders Peirce
  * [Precambrian rabbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Precambrian_rabbit "Precambrian rabbit") – Evolutionary biology hypothetical posed by J. B. S. Haldane
  * [Raven paradox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raven_paradox "Raven paradox") – Paradox arising from the question of what constitutes evidence for a statement
  * [Russell's teapot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russell%27s_teapot "Russell's teapot") – Analogy devised by Bertrand Russell
  * [Scientific method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_method "Scientific method") – Interplay between observation, experiment, and theory in science 
    * [Adversarial collaboration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adversarial_collaboration "Adversarial collaboration") – Method of research
    * [_Experimentum crucis_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Experimentum_crucis "Experimentum crucis") – Critical experiment
    * [Explanatory power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Explanatory_power "Explanatory power") – Ability of a theory to explain a subject
    * [Hypothetico-deductive model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypothetico-deductive_model "Hypothetico-deductive model") – Proposed description of the scientific method
    * [Models of scientific inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Models_of_scientific_inquiry "Models of scientific inquiry")
    * [Predictive power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Predictive_power "Predictive power") – Ability of a scientific theory to generate testable predictions
    * [Reproducibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reproducibility "Reproducibility") – Aspect of scientific research
    * [Statistical hypothesis testing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistical_hypothesis_testing "Statistical hypothesis testing") – Method of statistical inferencePages displaying short descriptions of redirect targets
    * [Superseded scientific theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superseded_scientific_theories "Superseded scientific theories") – Obsolete theories in natural history and natural philosophyPages displaying short descriptions of redirect targets
    * [Theory-ladenness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory-ladenness "Theory-ladenness") – Degree to which an observation is affected by one's presuppositions
  * [Scientific skepticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_skepticism "Scientific skepticism") – Questioning of claims lacking empirical evidence
  * [Superdeterminism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superdeterminism "Superdeterminism") – Class of theories in quantum mechanics
  * [Tautology (logic)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tautology_\(logic\) "Tautology \(logic\)") – In logic, a statement which is always true
  * [Trial and error](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_and_error "Trial and error") – Method of problem-solving


## Notes
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Falsifiability&action=edit&section=45 "Edit section: Notes")]
  1. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_ref-faithfultranslationofLoSD_1-0 "Jump up")** Popper wanted the main text of the 1959 English version, _The Logic of Scientific Discovery_ , to conform to the original and therefore refused to make substantial corrections, but only added notes and appendices and marked them with an asterisk. See [Popper 2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#CITEREFPopper2002), Translators' note
  2. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_ref-modeltheoryperspective_64-0 "Jump up")** This perspective can be found in any text on model theory. For example, see [Ebbinghaus 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#CITEREFEbbinghaus2017).
  3. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_ref-surveys_95-0 "Jump up")** Surveys were mailed to all active U.S. district court judges in November 1998 (N = 619). 303 usable surveys were obtained for a response rate of 51%. See [Krafka 2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#CITEREFKrafka2002), p. 9 in archived pdf.
  4. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_ref-102 "Jump up")** The Daubert case and subsequent cases that used it as a reference, including _[General Electric Co. v. Joiner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Electric_Co._v._Joiner "General Electric Co. v. Joiner")_ and _[Kumho Tire Co. v. Carmichael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kumho_Tire_Co._v._Carmichael "Kumho Tire Co. v. Carmichael")_ , resulted in an amendment of the [Federal Rules of Evidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Rules_of_Evidence "Federal Rules of Evidence")[[97]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTERules_of_Evidence201715Rule_702-100)[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_note-FOOTNOTERule_702_Notes2011-101)). The _Kumho Tire Co. v. Carmichael_ case and other cases considered the original _Daubert_ factors, but the amended rule, rule 702, even though it is often referred to as the [''Daubert'' standard](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=%27%27Daubert%27%27_standard&action=edit&redlink=1 "''Daubert'' standard \(page does not exist\)"), does not include the original Daubert factors or mention falsifiability or [testability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Testability "Testability") and neither does the majority opinion delivered by [William Rehnquist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Rehnquist "William Rehnquist") in the _General Electric Co. v. Joiner_ case.
  5. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability#cite_ref-104 "Jump up")** Not to be confused with [David Kaye (law professor)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Kaye_\(law_professor\) "David Kaye \(law professor\)"), United Nations special rapporteur. David H. Kaye is distinguished professor of law at [Penn State Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penn_State_Law "Penn State Law").
